People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, it's... it's mostly because of what's been going on down at the domino table lately. I go down there most afternoons, you know, just to sit with the other fellas at the park. I've been doing that for, oh, must be twenty years now since I stopped working. But lately, I've been noticing... well, they're giving me these little messages. Just small things they say when they're shuffling the tiles, looking at me a certain way, and I know they are meant for me. 

And it's not just them, either, which is the strange part. I listen to the radio in the mornings, just the local AM station while I have my coffee, and the announcer... he does the exact same thing. He slips these little messages in right after the weather report. My wife, she gets all worked up about it. She tells me, "Oh, it's just talk, you're making a mountain out of a molehill, they're just talking about the game or the news." But I know what I hear. I know they are warning me to be careful. 

A man gets to be my age, he knows when people are trying to tell him something important without coming right out and saying it. You have to read between the lines, you see? And I just... I know I need to be careful, even if my wife doesn't understand it. She's the one who said I should come talk to you about it, because she thinks it's just regular talk, but how could it be regular talk when the radio man knows exactly what to say to warn me? It's all connected, you see, and I just have to keep my guard up.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, now that you ask... it's a funny thing, really. You see, my wife, she goes over to her sister's house sometimes in the afternoons, or maybe she lays down for a nap in the back bedroom, and the house gets awful quiet. You know how an old house gets when it's just sitting there in the afternoon sun, not a sound except maybe the floorboards settling. And I'll be sitting there in my chair, just minding my own business, and I swear to you, doctor, I hear... well, I hear my old friend. 

We used to work together, years and years ago. But I'll hear him, plain as day, calling my name right from out on the front porch. Just calling out for me like he used to when he'd come by. And if he ain't calling my name, sometimes he's just humming. He was a big fellow, used to sing in the choir, and he had this real deep voice, and I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs. It is so clear, doctor. It sounds exactly like he is standing right there on the other side of the screen door. 

I can't even tell you how many times I've gotten up from my chair—which ain't easy with my knees these days, I tell you—and walked all the way over to the door to look out. I push the door open expecting to see him standing there on the porch planks, but... there ain't nobody there. Just the empty yard. But I know I heard him humming that tune. It's just as real as you talking to me right now. I don't tell my wife about it much, because she'd just say it's the wind or my ears acting up, but I know what I hear when the house is quiet like that.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really. I mean, my memory... it's about as good as you'd expect for a man my age, you know? I'm eighty-four, doctor, and when you get to be eighty-four, sure, maybe you don't remember every little thing like what you had for breakfast three Tuesdays ago. But as far as the important things? The daily tasks? No, I keep track of all that just fine. 

My wife, she handles a lot of the household stuff anyway, always has. She likes things done a certain way—she's particular about the laundry and how the kitchen is kept, you know how some women get when they've been running a house for fifty years. So I stay out of her way with that. But I know my routine. I get up, I have my coffee, I listen to the radio like I told you, and then in the afternoon, I head down to the park for the dominoes. I don't forget how to get to the park, I don't forget the rules of the game, and I certainly don't forget the faces of the fellas down there, even if they are acting a bit strange lately with those messages. 

And as for recent events, well, I remember what the radio man said just this morning! I remember it clear as a bell. So no, I wouldn't say my memory is going bad. My wife hasn't said anything about it either. She fusses at me about the radio and the park, sure, but she never says, "You're forgetting things, old man." She knows my mind is still sharp enough to catch on to what's going on around me, even if she doesn't want to admit it herself. I still know what's what.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, doctor, not really. I mean, I'm eighty-four years old, so you know how it is, the parts start to wear out a little bit. My knees, like I mentioned, they give me a bit of grief when it rains, or when I've been sitting too long in that chair of mine. It's a good chair, a recliner my son bought me for my seventy-fifth birthday, but if I stay in it too long, getting up is a real chore. You have to sort of rock yourself forward, you know? 

But as far as real medical conditions? Sicknesses? No, no, nothing like that. I've always been a pretty sturdy fellow. Worked hard all my life, kept moving, ate what my wife put in front of me—and she's a good cook, let me tell you, always made sure we had vegetables from the garden back when we used to keep a garden. We had tomatoes, cucumbers, squash... oh, the squash used to just take over the whole backyard if you didn't watch it. 

But no, I don't take a bunch of pills for my heart or my blood or anything like some of the fellas down at the domino table. You should see them, doctor, pulling out these little plastic boxes with the days of the week on them, popping pills right in the middle of a game. It's a wonder they can keep track of the tiles with all that medicine in them. But me? No, I'm healthy as a horse, aside from the creaky knees. I just go to the doctor for my regular checkups, and they always tell me I'm doing just fine for my age. So there's nothing physically wrong with me, no sir. Just trying to stay alert, you know, with everything else going on.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, feel as though what, doctor? I'm not quite sure where you're going with that one. You trailed off a bit there at the end. Do I ever feel as though... what? 

Are you asking if I feel like I'm getting older? Because I'll tell you, some mornings I wake up and I feel every bit of my eighty-four years, especially before I've had my coffee. My wife makes a good pot of coffee, always has. She uses one of those old percolators on the stove, none of this fancy push-button machine nonsense they have nowadays. You can hear it bubbling away while you're still in bed, and that smell just drifts all the way down the hall. It's the best way to wake up, I tell you. 

But as for feeling a certain way... I mean, I feel fine, mostly. Just a regular fellow trying to go about my day, listening to my radio, playing my dominoes down at the park with the fellas. I don't feel like anything strange is happening to *me* personally, if that's what you mean. It's just all those things happening *around* me, with the radio man and the people at the park trying to send those messages. But me, inside my own head? No, I feel just like I always have. So you might have to be a little more specific about what you're asking, doctor, because I'm just not quite catching your drift there.

Well, voices commenting on what I'm doing? No, no, it's nothing like that, doctor. It's not like there's a... a narrator in my head or something, telling me how I'm drinking my coffee or tying my shoes. That sounds like something out of one of those science fiction pictures my grandson likes to watch, the ones with the spaceships and the lasers. No, it's not like that at all. 

But, you know, sometimes... well, sometimes in the afternoons, when the house gets real quiet. My wife, she usually takes a little nap around two o'clock, right after we watch our stories on the television. She likes that one with the doctors, the young ones who are always getting into trouble. Anyway, she goes to lie down, and I'll be sitting in my chair, maybe reading the paper or just resting my eyes. And it's so quiet you can hear the clock ticking in the hallway. That old grandfather clock, we've had it since we got married, it was a gift from her uncle... 

But anyway, in that quiet, sometimes I hear... I hear my old friend, Charlie. Charlie passed away, oh, must be five years ago now. Good man, Charlie. We used to work together down at the plant, before it closed up. But sometimes, clear as day, I'll hear him call my name from out on the front porch. Just, "Hey there, old timer," just like he used to when he'd come over to sit and chew the fat. Or sometimes, it's not even him talking. Sometimes I'll just hear him humming. He used to sing in the choir down at the Methodist church on Elm Street, had this deep, booming baritone voice. And I'll hear him humming one of those old hymns, "How Great Thou Art" or "Rock of Ages," just humming it low and steady, right there on the porch. 

It sounds so real, doctor, like he's standing right on the other side of the screen door. I've even gotten up out of my chair a few times—and you know how hard that is for me with these knees—and walked over to look out the window. But of course, there's nobody there. Just the empty porch and Mrs. Higgins' cat sleeping on the railing. But I hear it. I hear him. It's not commenting on me, though. It's just... it's just Charlie, coming by for a visit, I suppose. Even if he's not really there. It's a comfort, in a way, hearing that old hymn. But no, nobody's telling me what I'm doing, if that's what you're asking. Just Charlie.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Do I what, doctor? You lost your train of thought again there. Just "Do you..." and then quiet. 

You know, my friend Arthur down at the park, he does that exact same thing. We'll be right in the middle of a game of 42—that's a domino game, doctor, very popular around here, been playing it since I was just a boy in short pants—and Arthur will just stop with a tile in his hand and say, "Did you..." and then just stare off into the distance. But with Arthur, we figure it's just his hearing aid acting up, or maybe he's trying to remember if he left his sprinkler on at the house. He's eighty-six, so he's got a couple of years on me, and his memory isn't quite what it used to be. 

But what were you going to ask? If you were going to ask if I... oh, I don't know, if I ever feel like folks are messing with my thoughts? Because I know I mentioned that radio man and the people at the park sending those messages, but they don't get *inside* my head. My thoughts are my own. 

Sometimes my wife, Martha, she'll say she knows exactly what I'm thinking before I even open my mouth. Like just yesterday, I was looking at a pecan pie she had sitting on the cooling rack on the kitchen counter, and she didn't even turn around from the sink, just said, "Don't you even think about it, Henry, that's for the church bake sale." But that's just because we've been married sixty-two years and she knows I've got a sweet tooth. It's not like she's actually seeing inside my brain or putting her thoughts into mine. Nobody can do that. A man's mind is his own private property, I've always said. What's up here under my hat stays under my hat, unless I decide to say it out loud. 

So, you'll have to finish your sentence, doctor. Do I what?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, if you're asking how long it's been going on, or how often... let me just sit and figure that out for a second. It's not like I marked it down on the calendar in the kitchen. Martha keeps a calendar right there by the icebox, one of those ones they give out for free down at the pharmacy with the pictures of the old covered bridges on it. She writes down all her doctor appointments and when the grandkids have their birthdays, and she even marks down when the trash pickup changes for the holidays. She's organized like that. But I didn't write any of this down, because you don't really think to write down when you hear an old friend humming, do you? 

But if I really put a pencil to it and think back... I'd say the voices—well, Charlie's humming, mostly—and those special messages from the radio man and the domino fellas, it's been happening on and off for about nine months now. 

Yes, nine months. I remember because it was right around the time we had that real bad cold snap last year. Our furnace was acting up, making this awful clanking noise down in the basement, and I had to get the repairman out here. Cost me an arm and a leg, too, and all he did was change a belt and clean a filter. But it was right around that same week when I first caught on to what the radio man was doing, and then Charlie started coming by the porch. 

It comes and goes, you see. It's not every single minute of the day, so it's not like it wears me out. It's just on and off. Sometimes I'll go a whole week or two and the radio is just playing the classic country station—George Jones and Tammy Wynette, just normal as pie—and the fellas at the park are just playing regular dominoes without any of the signaling. And the porch will be quiet as a mouse. But then, sure enough, it starts back up again, clear as a bell. So, nine months, give or take, of it coming and going like that. It's just become a regular part of things now, I suppose.

Over these... what? Over these nine months? You gotta finish your sentences, doc, or I'm gonna start thinking I need to check *your* hearing aid instead of the other way around! 

I figure you're asking how I've been feeling over these last nine months, right? If I've been feeling down in the dumps or blue about all this business with the radio man and Charlie humming on the porch. Well, if that's what you're getting at, the answer is no. I'm not one of those fellas who gets all sad and weepy. 

You know, my brother-in-law, Frank, now he got real depressed after he retired from the Union Pacific railroad. Worked there forty-two years, and the day they gave him his gold watch, it was like someone let all the air out of his tires. He just sat in his La-Z-Boy staring at the wood paneling, wouldn't even come out to the lake to fish for crappie when the weather was absolutely perfect for it. Martha had to take him over tuna casseroles just to make sure he was getting a hot meal, because his wife—that's Martha's sister, Betty—she was at her wit's end. 

But me? No, no, I don't get like that. I keep busy. I've got no reason to be moping around. I wake up every morning, I have my coffee—black, no sugar, because sugar rots your teeth and at eighty-four I'm proud to say I still have most of my own—and I read the paper. I got my yard work, even if it takes me three times as long to mow the front lawn now because of these knees. And I got my dominoes down at the park. 

I don't feel sad or hopeless or any of that. I just take things one day at a time. The Lord gives you the day, and you make the best of it, that's what my daddy used to say. He pulled me out of school in the eighth grade to help work the farm, so I learned real early on that you just gotta keep moving and not sit around feeling sorry for yourself. So no, I'm not depressed or crying or anything like that, over these nine months or any other months. I'm just living my life, same as always, just with a little extra company from Charlie's humming now and then.

I know they are warning me to be careful. \n\nA man gets to be my age, he knows when people are trying to tell him something important without coming right out and saying it. You have to read between the lines, you see? And I just... I know I need to be careful, even if my wife doesn't understand it. She's the one who said I should come talk to you about it, because she thinks it's just regular talk, but how could it be regular talk when the radio man knows exactly what to say to warn me? You see, my wife, she goes over to her sister's house sometimes in the afternoons, or maybe she lays down for a nap in the back bedroom, and the house gets awful quiet. You know how an old house gets when it's just sitting there in the afternoon sun, not a sound except maybe the floorboards settling. And I'll be sitting there in my chair, just minding my own business, and I swear to you, doctor, I hear... well, I hear my old friend. \n\nWe used to work together, years and years ago. But I'll hear him, plain as day, calling my name right from out on the front porch. Just calling out for me like he used to when he'd come by. And if he ain't calling my name, sometimes he's just humming. He was a big fellow, used to sing in the choir, and he had this real deep voice, and I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs. It is so clear, doctor. It sounds exactly like he is standing right there on the other side of the screen door. \n\nI can't even tell you how many times I've gotten up from my chair—which ain't easy with my knees these days, I tell you—and walked all the way over to the door to look out. I push the door open expecting to see him standing there on the porch planks, but... there ain't nobody there. Just the empty yard. But I know I heard him humming that tune. I don't tell my wife about it much, because she'd just say it's the wind or my ears acting up, but I know what I hear when the house is quiet like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you or your wife noticed any changes in your memory, such as forgetting recent events or having trouble keeping track of daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, my memory... it's about as good as you'd expect for a man my age, you know? I'm eighty-four, doctor, and when you get to be eighty-four, sure, maybe you don't remember every little thing like what you had for breakfast three Tuesdays ago. But as far as the important things? The daily tasks? No, I keep track of all that just fine. \n\nMy wife, she handles a lot of the household stuff anyway, always has. She likes things done a certain way—she's particular about the laundry and how the kitchen is kept, you know how some women get when they've been running a house for fifty years. So I stay out of her way with that. But I know my routine. I get up, I have my coffee, I listen to the radio like I told you, and then in the afternoon, I head down to the park for the dominoes. I don't forget how to get to the park, I don't forget the rules of the game, and I certainly don't forget the faces of the fellas down there, even if they are acting a bit strange lately with those messages. \n\nAnd as for recent events, well, I remember what the radio man said just this morning! I remember it clear as a bell. So no, I wouldn't say my memory is going bad. My wife hasn't said anything about it either. She fusses at me about the radio and the park, sure, but she never says, \"You're forgetting things, old man.\" She knows my mind is still sharp enough to catch on to what's going on around me, even if she doesn't want to admit it herself. Do I ever... well, there you go again, doc. Trailing off like a loose wagon wheel. "Do you ever..." Do I ever what? See things? Is that what you're trying to spit out? 

Because if you're wondering if I'm seeing ghosts or pink elephants dancing on the rug, the answer is no. My eyes are just fine. Well, I've got cataracts, Dr. Miller down at the eye clinic told me that last spring. Said they're "ripening" like a pair of Georgia peaches and we'll have to scrape 'em eventually, but I still drive during the daylight and I certainly don't see anything that isn't supposed to be there. 

But if you're asking about Charlie again, since we were just talking about his humming... like I said, it's just the hearing. It usually happens some afternoons, when the house is real quiet. Martha likes to lay down for a nap around two o'clock, right when her programs come on the television, she just dozes right off in her recliner. And when the house gets quiet like that, I hear my old friend call my name from the porch. Just "Henry!" loud and clear. 

Or sometimes he'll just hum a choir song. Charlie and I, we used to sing in the First Baptist choir together back in the seventies, he had the most beautiful baritone voice you ever heard, could really carry the low notes on "How Great Thou Art." And I'll hear him humming one of those old hymns, clear like he is standing right there on the wood planks next to the screen door. 

I'll get up—which takes a minute with these knees, I have to rock back and forth a bit to get my momentum—and I'll look out the window, but there's nobody there. Just my rocking chair and Martha's hanging ferns catching the breeze. But the sound of it, the calling my name and the humming, it's as real as you sitting there not finishing your sentences. So no, I don't see anything, if that's what you were getting at. I just hear him.

He slips these little messages in right after the weather report. My wife, she gets all worked up about it. She tells me, \"Oh, it's just talk, you're making a mountain out of a molehill, they're just talking about the game or the news.\" But I know what I hear. I know they are warning me to be careful. \n\nA man gets to be my age, he knows when people are trying to tell him something important without coming right out and saying it. You see, my wife, she goes over to her sister's house sometimes in the afternoons, or maybe she lays down for a nap in the back bedroom, and the house gets awful quiet. You know how an old house gets when it's just sitting there in the afternoon sun, not a sound except maybe the floorboards settling. And I'll be sitting there in my chair, just minding my own business, and I swear to you, doctor, I hear... well, I hear my old friend. \n\nWe used to work together, years and years ago. But I'll hear him, plain as day, calling my name right from out on the front porch. Just calling out for me like he used to when he'd come by. And if he ain't calling my name, sometimes he's just humming. He was a big fellow, used to sing in the choir, and he had this real deep voice, and I'll hear him humming one of those old choir songs. It is so clear, doctor. It sounds exactly like he is standing right there on the other side of the screen door. \n\nI can't even tell you how many times I've gotten up from my chair—which ain't easy with my knees these days, I tell you—and walked all the way over to the door to look out. The daily tasks? No, I keep track of all that just fine. \n\nMy wife, she handles a lot of the household stuff anyway, always has. She likes things done a certain way—she's particular about the laundry and how the kitchen is kept, you know how some women get when they've been running a house for fifty years. So I stay out of her way with that. But I know my routine. I get up, I have my coffee, I listen to the radio like I told you, and then in the afternoon, I head down to the park for the dominoes. I don't forget how to get to the park, I don't forget the rules of the game, and I certainly don't forget the faces of the fellas down there, even if they are acting a bit strange lately with those messages. \n\nAnd as for recent events, well, I remember what the radio man said just this morning! I remember it clear as a bell. So no, I wouldn't say my memory is going bad. My wife hasn't said anything about it either. She fusses at me about the radio and the park, sure, but she never says, \"You're forgetting things, old man.\" She knows my mind is still sharp enough to catch on to what's going on around me, even if she doesn't want to admit it herself. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Do you ever feel... Lord have mercy, doc, are we paying by the word today? "Do you ever feel..." Feel what? Like a spring chicken? Like a million bucks? Because I can tell you right now, with these knees acting the way they do when the barometric pressure drops, I feel more like a rusty old John Deere tractor most mornings. 

But I'm guessing you mean something else with all these questions. Are you trying to ask if I feel like... I don't know, like someone's messing with my head or making me do things? Like I'm some kind of puppet on a string? Because if that's what's in that little notebook of yours, you are barking up the wrong tree entirely. 

Nobody controls Henry. Not now, not ever. Martha will be the very first one to tell you that. We've been married fifty-eight years this coming August—we had our reception down at the VFW hall, best pot roast I ever ate in my life, her mother made it—and she gave up trying to boss me around back in nineteen-seventy-two. I do what I want, when I want. I drive my own self down to the hardware store when I need a new washer for the garden hose, I mow my own lawn, and I make my own decisions. Always have. I've been my own man since my daddy had me out there running the thresher before I even had hair on my chin. 

So no, I don't feel like anyone or anything is pulling my strings or putting thoughts in my head or moving my arms around for me, if that's what you're trying to get at. I'm the only one steering this ship, plain and simple. Now, did you have another question, or are you gonna leave me hanging on the edge of my seat again?
